18Business and Data StorytellingWhat → So What → Now What turns a report into a story; one named person moves people more than one large statistic.
19Your Personal Story BankStories are found, not invented - keep a story journal, pick your message first, then choose the true story that proves it.
20Writing to Be ReadBLUF, short words and sentences, active voice, one idea per paragraph, cut at least 10% of the draft.
21AIDA, PAS, and BABMatch the framework to the reader's state: cold and unaware (AIDA), busy and skeptical (PAS), or ready to re-engage (BAB).
22Emails, Proposals, and MessagesPoint first, proof second; specific subject lines; one ask per message with a deadline, not "let me know your thoughts."
23Rhetoric on the PageConcrete beats abstract, tricolon and antithesis add shape, "you" beats "we" - rhetoric is salt, not the meal.
24Arguments and EvidenceClaim + Evidence + Warrant, mixed evidence types, steelman the best objection, name fallacies instead of just shouting "gotcha."
25Listening and RapportValidate the feeling before fixing the problem; mirror the last few words; open questions to explore, closed questions to confirm.
26Difficult Conversations and ConflictFacts before stories, "I feel X when Y because Z, I'd like W," and restore safety before continuing the topic.
27Executive PresenceRecommendation first, translate updates into money/time/risk/customers - gravitas is 67% of executive presence, appearance just 5%.
28Negotiation and Everyday PersuasionArgue interests not positions, know your BATNA, anchor first when you know the range, label emotions then go silent.

The Core Frameworks

Ethos / Pathos / Logos (Ch. 3)

LeverWhat it isHow to build it
EthosCredibility, trustWarmth first, competence second; show track record honestly
PathosEmotional connectionStories, stakes, a character the listener cares about
LogosLogical reasoningEvidence, numbers, clear cause-and-effect
KairosThe right momentAsk: which lever, and is now the time?

Cialdini's 7 Principles of Persuasion (Ch. 5)

PrincipleOne-line trigger
ReciprocityGive first, personalized, unprompted
Commitment & ConsistencyGet it said out loud, especially publicly
Social ProofShow what similar others already do (descriptive norms)
LikingSimilarity, compliments, cooperation
AuthorityCredentials and expertise, signaled honestly
ScarcityReal limits on time or supply
Unity"We're on the same team" - shared identity

AIDA / PAS / BAB (Ch. 21)

FrameworkStepsBest for
AIDAAttention → Interest → Desire → ActionCold, unaware readers
PASProblem → Agitate → SolveBusy, skeptical B2B readers
BABBefore → After → BridgeRe-engagement, culture change, hopeful asks

PREP (Ch. 14)

StepPurpose
PointState your answer or opinion up front
ReasonGive the "because"
ExampleMake it concrete with one instance
PointRestate the point to close the loop

SBI (Situation-Behavior-Impact)

StepSay
SituationWhen/where this happened
BehaviorWhat was observed, factually - no interpretation
ImpactThe effect it had on you, the work, or the team

STAR (Ch. 17, 19)

StepSayNote
SituationBrief contextKeep short
TaskWhat you had to achieveKeep short
ActionWhat you did~60% of the answer; say "I," not "we"
ResultThe outcome, ideally quantifiedClose strong

SUCCESs (Ch. 16)

LetterIngredient
SSimple
UUnexpected
CConcrete
CCredible
EEmotional
SStories

Golden Rules

  • Judge every message by two questions: did they get it, and did they do something about it.
  • Lead with the point - BLUF, every time, in speech and in writing.
  • Understand the audience before you try to move them; empathy is the engine, technique is the vehicle.
  • Use all three levers - ethos, pathos, logos - and check which one you're overusing.
  • Open with a story to earn attention, close with data to prove it isn't a one-off.
  • One idea per slide, one ask per message, one core sentence per talk.
  • Pause instead of filling silence - it costs nothing and does three jobs at once.
  • Before any high-stakes ask, run the 4-question ethics test: intent, transparency, mutual benefit, freedom to say no.

Common Mistakes to Avoid

  • Explaining from your own starting point instead of the listener's (the curse of knowledge).
  • Overusing your favorite lever - all logos, or all pathos, with nothing else.
  • Burying the recommendation at the end of an email, update, or talk.
  • Faking scarcity, urgency, or reciprocity - it works once, then trust doesn't come back to full strength.
  • Treating slides as the script or the document instead of a visual aid.
  • Answering the question you wish they'd asked instead of the one they actually asked.
  • Skipping the repair conversation after conflict - the conflict itself rarely breaks trust, the silence afterward does.
  • Jumping straight from fact to fix in a difficult conversation without validating the feeling first.

90-Day Practice Plan

WeeksFocusWhat to actually practice
1-4
Foundations		Clarity & structureWrite every email BLUF-first and cut 10% before sending. Draft one talk using the one-sentence/three-points method. Practice naming the type of noise (physical/psychological/semantic) in three real conversations.
5-8
Delivery & persuasion		Voice, body, and the persuasion leversRecord yourself weekly and work on pace, pause, and pitch. Run the ethos/pathos/logos check on one real pitch or proposal. Apply one Cialdini principle deliberately and ethically in a real ask. Build your personal story bank - write down three stories using STAR or hero-complication-resolution.
9-12
Real-world stakes		Difficult conversations & high-stakes momentsUse SBI or "I feel X when Y because Z, I'd like W" in one real difficult conversation. Practice PREP for impromptu Q&A at every meeting. Negotiate one real thing using interests-not-positions and a clear BATNA. Deliver one talk or update using everything above end to end.
How to use this page: Don't try to apply all 28 chapters at once. Pick one framework per week, use it in a real conversation or piece of writing within 48 hours, and only then move to the next. Skill sticks through repetition under real stakes, not through re-reading.
